If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to something that isn't a bat but might remind you of one, like a picture or a toy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if it looks enough like one, maybe. I mostly notice it at work because that’s where it’s come up. I don’t think it really happens much at home or outside… or at least nothing that stands out to me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of being bitten or harmed by bats, or is it more about the appearance or presence of bats in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more the presence of them, or even just the look of them. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t actually going to hurt me, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic… but if I see something that looks like one, or even a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind just reacts like I’m in real danger. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to other animals that are not bats but have similar characteristics, such as rodents or other small creatures?\n\nPatient: Not really with rodents, I don’t think. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks. Um… it’s probably not a huge thing, but at work, if I even think there might be a bat or something flapping around in the stockroom, I kind of react right away. My heart jumps, I start sweating, and I feel like I need to get out of there immediately. I know it might sound silly, but that’s what’s been bringing me in.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically bats that trigger this reaction, or would any animal or unexpected movement cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: Mostly bats, yeah. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me or anything, but if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my brain kind of treats it like real danger right away. I’m not trying to overreact—it just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you react similarly to other animals that fly, like birds or insects?\n\nPatient: Not really birds or bugs, no… at least not in the same way. It’s more when I think it’s a bat, or something looks like one. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t actually going to hurt me, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic… but if I see something that looks like one, or even a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind just reacts like I’m in real danger. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to other animals that are not bats but have similar characteristics, such as rodents or other small creatures?\n\nPatient: Not really with rodents, I don’t think. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats more about how they look or more about the fact that they are present in your space?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but mostly the fact that it’s there in my space, if that makes sense. It’s more if something looks like a bat, or moves like one. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see that shape or just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps straight to “danger.” I’m not sure if that counts as “besides bats,” but that’s the closest I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation where something that is not a bat, but maybe reminds you of one in some way, triggers a similar fear response in you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I know it might sound silly, but that’s what’s been bringing me in.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically bats that trigger this reaction, or would any animal or unexpected movement cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: Mostly bats, yeah. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me or anything, but if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my brain kind of treats it like real danger right away. I’m not trying to overreact—it just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you react similarly to other animals that fly, like birds or insects?\n\nPatient: Not really birds or bugs, no… at least not in the same way. It’s more when I think it’s a bat, or something looks like one. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t actually going to hurt me, but if I catch a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind just treats it like I’m in real danger. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to something that isn't a bat but might remind you of one, like a picture or a toy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if it looks enough like one, maybe. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, logically, but if I see something that even resembles one—or just a quick flutter in the back hallway—my mind kind of jumps to “danger” right away. I realize that might sound like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Does your reaction to bats or things that resemble bats happen in other locations besides work, like at home or outdoors?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mostly notice it at work because that’s where it’s come up. I don’t think it really happens much at home or outside… or at least nothing that stands out to me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of being bitten or harmed by bats, or is it more about the appearance or presence of bats in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more the presence of them, or even just the look of them. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t actually going to hurt me, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic… but if I see something that looks like one, or even a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind just reacts like I’m in real danger. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to other animals that are not bats but have similar characteristics, such as rodents or other small creatures?\n\nPatient: Not really with rodents, I don’t think. It’s more when something has that bat-like look or movement… like if I catch a quick flutter in the back hallway, or see something that looks like one, my brain kind of treats it like danger even though I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats more about how they look or more about the fact that they are present in your space?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but mostly the fact that it’s there in my space, if that makes sense. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind reacts like I’m in real danger. I know that probably sounds a little much, but that’s how it feels in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything else, besides bats, that might trigger a similar reaction in you?\n\nPatient: Maybe not a whole different thing, exactly. It’s more if something looks like a bat, or moves like one. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see that shape or just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps straight to “danger.” I’m not sure if that counts as “besides bats,” but that’s the closest I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation where something that is not a bat, but maybe reminds you of one in some way, triggers a similar fear response in you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not really another animal, exactly, but if I see something that has that same sort of shape, or if there’s just a quick flutter in the back hallway, I react almost like it’s a bat. I know, logically, a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but in that moment my mind treats it like I’m in real danger. I know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be the same if you knew for certain that the flutter you saw was not a bat, but something else that looked similar?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, if I knew for sure it wasn’t a bat… but honestly, I think I’d still react at first. Like if I saw that quick flutter or something with the same shape in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps before I can talk myself down. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me anyway, but in that moment it still feels like danger, even if part of me knows better. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats because of how they look or because they are present in your environment?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the way they look, or even just that quick fluttering movement. I mean, if an actual bat were in the store, that would definitely set me off, but even something that just looks like one in the back hallway can make my mind jump to danger. I know it probably isn’t really going to hurt me, so I feel a little silly saying it, but that’s how it hits me at first.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me or anything, but if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my brain kind of treats it like real danger right away. I’m not trying to overreact—it just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you react similarly to other animals that fly, like birds or insects?\n\nPatient: Not really birds or bugs, no… at least not in the same way. It’s more when I think it’s a bat, or something looks like one. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t actually going to hurt me, but if I catch a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind just treats it like I’m in real danger. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to something that isn't a bat but might remind you of one, like a picture or a toy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if it looks enough like one, maybe. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, logically, but if I see something that even resembles one—or just a quick flutter in the back hallway—my mind kind of jumps to “danger” right away. I realize that might sound like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Does your reaction to bats or things that resemble bats happen in other locations besides work, like at home or outdoors?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mostly notice it at work because that’s where it’s come up. I don’t think it really happens much at home or outside… or at least nothing that stands out to me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of being bitten or harmed by bats, or is it more about the appearance or presence of bats in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more the presence of them, or even just the look of them. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t actually going to hurt me, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic… but if I see something that looks like one, or even a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind just reacts like I’m in real danger. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to other animals that are not bats but have similar characteristics, such as rodents or other small creatures?\n\nPatient: Not really with rodents, I don’t think. It’s more when something has that bat-like look or movement… like if I catch a quick flutter in the back hallway, or see something that looks like one, my brain kind of treats it like danger even though I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats more about how they look or more about the fact that they are present in your space?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but mostly the fact that it’s there in my space, if that makes sense. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind reacts like I’m in real danger. I know that probably sounds a little much, but that’s how it feels in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything else, besides bats, that might trigger a similar reaction in you?\n\nPatient: Maybe not a whole different thing, exactly. It’s more if something looks like a bat, or moves like one. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see that shape or just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps straight to “danger.” I’m not sure if that counts as “besides bats,” but that’s the closest I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation where something that is not a bat, but maybe reminds you of one in some way, triggers a similar fear response in you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not really another animal, exactly, but if I see something that has that same sort of shape, or if there’s just a quick flutter in the back hallway, I react almost like it’s a bat. I know, logically, a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but in that moment my mind treats it like I’m in real danger. I know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be the same if you knew for certain that the flutter you saw was not a bat, but something else that looked similar?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, if I knew for sure it wasn’t a bat… but honestly, I think I’d still react at first. Like if I saw that quick flutter or something with the same shape in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps before I can talk myself down. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me anyway, but in that moment it still feels like danger, even if part of me knows better. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats because of how they look or because they are present in your environment?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the way they look, or even just that quick fluttering movement. I mean, if an actual bat were in the store, that would definitely set me off, but even something that just looks like one in the back hallway can make my mind jump to danger. I know it probably isn’t really going to hurt me, so I feel a little silly saying it, but that’s how it hits me at first.\n\nDoctor: Would you react with fear to a toy or replica of a bat, even if you knew it was not real?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably, at least at first. I mean, if I knew it was just a toy, I don’t think I’d completely lose it or anything, but if it had that same bat shape, or if I caught it quickly out of the corner of my eye, my body would still kind of jump.\n\nIt’s like my mind reacts before I can remind myself it’s not real. I know a bat in the store probably wouldn’t even hurt me, but anything that looks like one can make me feel like I’m in danger for a moment. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of what a bat could do to you, like biting or disease, or is it just the presence or appearance of the bat itself that scares you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about, like, “what if it bites me” or disease, not mostly anyway. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me.\n\nIt’s more that if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter back in the hallway, my mind treats it like I’m in real danger before I can really think it through. It sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s honestly how it feels at first.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t actually going to hurt me, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic… but if I see something that looks like one, or even a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind just reacts like I’m in real danger. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to other animals that are not bats but have similar characteristics, such as rodents or other small creatures?\n\nPatient: Not really with rodents, I don’t think. It’s more when something has that bat-like look or movement… like if I catch a quick flutter in the back hallway, or see something that looks like one, my brain kind of treats it like danger even though I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats more about how they look or more about the fact that they are present in your space?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but mostly the fact that it’s there in my space, if that makes sense. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind reacts like I’m in real danger. I know that probably sounds a little much, but that’s how it feels in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything else, besides bats, that might trigger a similar reaction in you?\n\nPatient: Maybe not a whole different thing, exactly. It’s more if something looks like a bat, or moves like one. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see that shape or just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps straight to “danger.” I’m not sure if that counts as “besides bats,” but that’s the closest I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation where something that is not a bat, but maybe reminds you of one in some way, triggers a similar fear response in you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not really another animal, exactly, but if I see something that has that same sort of shape, or if there’s just a quick flutter in the back hallway, I react almost like it’s a bat. I know, logically, a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but in that moment my mind treats it like I’m in real danger. I know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be the same if you knew for certain that the flutter you saw was not a bat, but something else that looked similar?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, if I knew for sure it wasn’t a bat… but honestly, I think I’d still react at first. Like if I saw that quick flutter or something with the same shape in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps before I can talk myself down. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me anyway, but in that moment it still feels like danger, even if part of me knows better. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats because of how they look or because they are present in your environment?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the way they look, or even just that quick fluttering movement. I mean, if an actual bat were in the store, that would definitely set me off, but even something that just looks like one in the back hallway can make my mind jump to danger. I know it probably isn’t really going to hurt me, so I feel a little silly saying it, but that’s how it hits me at first.\n\nDoctor: Would you react with fear to a toy or replica of a bat, even if you knew it was not real?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably, at least at first. I mean, if I knew it was just a toy, I don’t think I’d completely lose it or anything, but if it had that same bat shape, or if I caught it quickly out of the corner of my eye, my body would still kind of jump.\n\nIt’s like my mind reacts before I can remind myself it’s not real. I know a bat in the store probably wouldn’t even hurt me, but anything that looks like one can make me feel like I’m in danger for a moment. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of what a bat could do to you, like biting or disease, or is it just the presence or appearance of the bat itself that scares you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about, like, “what if it bites me” or disease, not mostly anyway. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me.\n\nIt’s more that if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter back in the hallway, my mind treats it like I’m in real danger before I can really think it through. It sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s honestly how it feels at first.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be less intense if you saw a picture of a bat, rather than a real bat or something that looks like one in person?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think a picture would probably be less intense. I might still feel uncomfortable if it looked really realistic, but it’s not the same as seeing something in person or catching that quick flutter in the back hallway.\n\nWhen it’s right there, even if I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, my mind just kind of treats it like I’m in danger before I can talk myself down. A picture gives me a little more distance, I guess. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats more about how they look or more about the fact that they are present in your space?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but mostly the fact that it’s there in my space, if that makes sense. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind reacts like I’m in real danger. I know that probably sounds a little much, but that’s how it feels in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything else, besides bats, that might trigger a similar reaction in you?\n\nPatient: Maybe not a whole different thing, exactly. It’s more if something looks like a bat, or moves like one. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see that shape or just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps straight to “danger.” I’m not sure if that counts as “besides bats,” but that’s the closest I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation where something that is not a bat, but maybe reminds you of one in some way, triggers a similar fear response in you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not really another animal, exactly, but if I see something that has that same sort of shape, or if there’s just a quick flutter in the back hallway, I react almost like it’s a bat. I know, logically, a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but in that moment my mind treats it like I’m in real danger. I know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be the same if you knew for certain that the flutter you saw was not a bat, but something else that looked similar?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, if I knew for sure it wasn’t a bat… but honestly, I think I’d still react at first. Like if I saw that quick flutter or something with the same shape in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps before I can talk myself down. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me anyway, but in that moment it still feels like danger, even if part of me knows better. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats because of how they look or because they are present in your environment?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the way they look, or even just that quick fluttering movement. I mean, if an actual bat were in the store, that would definitely set me off, but even something that just looks like one in the back hallway can make my mind jump to danger. I know it probably isn’t really going to hurt me, so I feel a little silly saying it, but that’s how it hits me at first.\n\nDoctor: Would you react with fear to a toy or replica of a bat, even if you knew it was not real?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably, at least at first. I mean, if I knew it was just a toy, I don’t think I’d completely lose it or anything, but if it had that same bat shape, or if I caught it quickly out of the corner of my eye, my body would still kind of jump.\n\nIt’s like my mind reacts before I can remind myself it’s not real. I know a bat in the store probably wouldn’t even hurt me, but anything that looks like one can make me feel like I’m in danger for a moment. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of what a bat could do to you, like biting or disease, or is it just the presence or appearance of the bat itself that scares you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about, like, “what if it bites me” or disease, not mostly anyway. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me.\n\nIt’s more that if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter back in the hallway, my mind treats it like I’m in real danger before I can really think it through. It sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s honestly how it feels at first.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be less intense if you saw a picture of a bat, rather than a real bat or something that looks like one in person?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think a picture would probably be less intense. I might still feel uncomfortable if it looked really realistic, but it’s not the same as seeing something in person or catching that quick flutter in the back hallway.\n\nWhen it’s right there, even if I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, my mind just kind of treats it like I’m in danger before I can talk myself down. A picture gives me a little more distance, I guess. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear of bats is something that you think about or worry about even when you're not at work or in a situation where you might encounter a bat?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I don’t sit around thinking about bats all the time or anything. It’s more when something reminds me of them, or if I know I’m in a place where one could show up.\n\nOutside of work, I don’t think I worry about it much. At least, not that I’ve really noticed. I hope that answers it the right way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t actually going to hurt me, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic… but if I see something that looks like one, or even a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind just reacts like I’m in real danger. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to other animals that are not bats but have similar characteristics, such as rodents or other small creatures?\n\nPatient: Not really with rodents, I don’t think. It’s more when something has that bat-like look or movement… like if I catch a quick flutter in the back hallway, or see something that looks like one, my brain kind of treats it like danger even though I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats more about how they look or more about the fact that they are present in your space?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but mostly the fact that it’s there in my space, if that makes sense. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind reacts like I’m in real danger. I know that probably sounds a little much, but that’s how it feels in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything else, besides bats, that might trigger a similar reaction in you?\n\nPatient: Maybe not a whole different thing, exactly. It’s more if something looks like a bat, or moves like one. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see that shape or just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps straight to “danger.” I’m not sure if that counts as “besides bats,” but that’s the closest I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation where something that is not a bat, but maybe reminds you of one in some way, triggers a similar fear response in you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not really another animal, exactly, but if I see something that has that same sort of shape, or if there’s just a quick flutter in the back hallway, I react almost like it’s a bat. I know, logically, a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but in that moment my mind treats it like I’m in real danger. I know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be the same if you knew for certain that the flutter you saw was not a bat, but something else that looked similar?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, if I knew for sure it wasn’t a bat… but honestly, I think I’d still react at first. Like if I saw that quick flutter or something with the same shape in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps before I can talk myself down. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me anyway, but in that moment it still feels like danger, even if part of me knows better. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats because of how they look or because they are present in your environment?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the way they look, or even just that quick fluttering movement. I mean, if an actual bat were in the store, that would definitely set me off, but even something that just looks like one in the back hallway can make my mind jump to danger. I know it probably isn’t really going to hurt me, so I feel a little silly saying it, but that’s how it hits me at first.\n\nDoctor: Would you react with fear to a toy or replica of a bat, even if you knew it was not real?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably, at least at first. I mean, if I knew it was just a toy, I don’t think I’d completely lose it or anything, but if it had that same bat shape, or if I caught it quickly out of the corner of my eye, my body would still kind of jump.\n\nIt’s like my mind reacts before I can remind myself it’s not real. I know a bat in the store probably wouldn’t even hurt me, but anything that looks like one can make me feel like I’m in danger for a moment. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of what a bat could do to you, like biting or disease, or is it just the presence or appearance of the bat itself that scares you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about, like, “what if it bites me” or disease, not mostly anyway. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me.\n\nIt’s more that if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter back in the hallway, my mind treats it like I’m in real danger before I can really think it through. It sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s honestly how it feels at first.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be less intense if you saw a picture of a bat, rather than a real bat or something that looks like one in person?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think a picture would probably be less intense. I might still feel uncomfortable if it looked really realistic, but it’s not the same as seeing something in person or catching that quick flutter in the back hallway.\n\nWhen it’s right there, even if I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, my mind just kind of treats it like I’m in danger before I can talk myself down. A picture gives me a little more distance, I guess. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear of bats is something that you think about or worry about even when you're not at work or in a situation where you might encounter a bat?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I don’t sit around thinking about bats all the time or anything. It’s more when something reminds me of them, or if I know I’m in a place where one could show up.\n\nOutside of work, I don’t think I worry about it much. At least, not that I’ve really noticed. I hope that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself feeling afraid or anxious about bats when you're not in a situation where you might see one, just because you're thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Not usually just sitting at home thinking about them, no. I mean… if the thought pops in my head, it’s uncomfortable, but what really gets me is when I think there might actually be one around.\n\nLike at work, if there’s something flapping in the stockroom or I even think it could be a bat, my heart kind of jumps right away, I start sweating, and I feel like I need to get out of there immediately. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s the part that brings me in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t actually going to hurt me, but if I catch a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind just treats it like I’m in real danger. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to something that isn't a bat but might remind you of one, like a picture or a toy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if it looks enough like one, maybe. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, logically, but if I see something that even resembles one—or just a quick flutter in the back hallway—my mind kind of jumps to “danger” right away. I realize that might sound like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Does your reaction to bats or things that resemble bats happen in other locations besides work, like at home or outdoors?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mostly notice it at work because that’s where it’s come up. I don’t think it really happens much at home or outside… or at least nothing that stands out to me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of being bitten or harmed by bats, or is it more about the appearance or presence of bats in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s more the presence of them, or even just the look of them. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t actually going to hurt me, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic… but if I see something that looks like one, or even a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind just reacts like I’m in real danger. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar reaction to other animals that are not bats but have similar characteristics, such as rodents or other small creatures?\n\nPatient: Not really with rodents, I don’t think. It’s more when something has that bat-like look or movement… like if I catch a quick flutter in the back hallway, or see something that looks like one, my brain kind of treats it like danger even though I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats more about how they look or more about the fact that they are present in your space?\n\nPatient: I think it’s kind of both, but mostly the fact that it’s there in my space, if that makes sense. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind reacts like I’m in real danger. I know that probably sounds a little much, but that’s how it feels in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of anything else, besides bats, that might trigger a similar reaction in you?\n\nPatient: Maybe not a whole different thing, exactly. It’s more if something looks like a bat, or moves like one. Like, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see that shape or just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps straight to “danger.” I’m not sure if that counts as “besides bats,” but that’s the closest I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation where something that is not a bat, but maybe reminds you of one in some way, triggers a similar fear response in you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not really another animal, exactly, but if I see something that has that same sort of shape, or if there’s just a quick flutter in the back hallway, I react almost like it’s a bat. I know, logically, a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but in that moment my mind treats it like I’m in real danger. I know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be the same if you knew for certain that the flutter you saw was not a bat, but something else that looked similar?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, if I knew for sure it wasn’t a bat… but honestly, I think I’d still react at first. Like if I saw that quick flutter or something with the same shape in the back hallway, my mind kind of jumps before I can talk myself down. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me anyway, but in that moment it still feels like danger, even if part of me knows better. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is your reaction to bats because of how they look or because they are present in your environment?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the way they look, or even just that quick fluttering movement. I mean, if an actual bat were in the store, that would definitely set me off, but even something that just looks like one in the back hallway can make my mind jump to danger. I know it probably isn’t really going to hurt me, so I feel a little silly saying it, but that’s how it hits me at first.\n\nDoctor: Would you react with fear to a toy or replica of a bat, even if you knew it was not real?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably, at least at first. I mean, if I knew it was just a toy, I don’t think I’d completely lose it or anything, but if it had that same bat shape, or if I caught it quickly out of the corner of my eye, my body would still kind of jump.\n\nIt’s like my mind reacts before I can remind myself it’s not real. I know a bat in the store probably wouldn’t even hurt me, but anything that looks like one can make me feel like I’m in danger for a moment. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of what a bat could do to you, like biting or disease, or is it just the presence or appearance of the bat itself that scares you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about, like, “what if it bites me” or disease, not mostly anyway. I mean, I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me.\n\nIt’s more that if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter back in the hallway, my mind treats it like I’m in real danger before I can really think it through. It sounds a little dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s honestly how it feels at first.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be less intense if you saw a picture of a bat, rather than a real bat or something that looks like one in person?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think a picture would probably be less intense. I might still feel uncomfortable if it looked really realistic, but it’s not the same as seeing something in person or catching that quick flutter in the back hallway.\n\nWhen it’s right there, even if I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, my mind just kind of treats it like I’m in danger before I can talk myself down. A picture gives me a little more distance, I guess. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear of bats is something that you think about or worry about even when you're not at work or in a situation where you might encounter a bat?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I don’t sit around thinking about bats all the time or anything. It’s more when something reminds me of them, or if I know I’m in a place where one could show up.\n\nOutside of work, I don’t think I worry about it much. At least, not that I’ve really noticed. I hope that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself feeling afraid or anxious about bats when you're not in a situation where you might see one, just because you're thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Not usually just sitting at home thinking about them, no. I mean… if the thought pops in my head, it’s uncomfortable, but what really gets me is when I think there might actually be one around.\n\nLike at work, if there’s something flapping in the stockroom or I even think it could be a bat, my heart kind of jumps right away, I start sweating, and I feel like I need to get out of there immediately. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s the part that brings me in.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else that can trigger this fear response in you besides bats or things that resemble bats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve really noticed. It’s mostly bats, or something that my brain reads as a bat for a second.\n\nI guess if something suddenly flutters near me, that can make me jump, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. I’m not trying to make it sound worse than it is—it’s really the bat part that seems to set it off. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
